<a id='question-098'></a>

### Question 098: Cardiac Pathology: Focus 02-48 (Physiology)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the pathophysiology of hypertrophic cardiomyopathy (HCM), specifically the role of mutations in sarcomeric proteins leading to diastolic dysfunction and potential outflow tract obstruction. (理解肥厚型心肌病的病理生理学，特别是肌节蛋白突变导致舒张功能障碍和潜在流出道梗阻的作用。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 45-year-old male marathon runner presents to the emergency department complaining of sudden onset dyspnea, chest pain, and near-syncope during a race. He has a known history of hypertension, managed with lisinopril, and hyperlipidemia, treated with atorvastatin. His father died suddenly at age 50 during exercise. On examination, his blood pressure is 140/80 mmHg, heart rate is 110 bpm, and respiratory rate is 24 breaths/min. Auscultation reveals a harsh systolic murmur that increases with Valsalva maneuver and decreases with squatting. An ECG shows left ventricular hypertrophy (LVH) and T-wave inversions. An echocardiogram demonstrates asymmetric septal hypertrophy (septal thickness 1.8 cm, free wall thickness 1.2 cm), systolic anterior motion (SAM) of the mitral valve, and left ventricular outflow tract (LVOT) obstruction with a peak gradient of 50 mmHg at rest. Cardiac MRI confirms the echocardiogram findings and shows myocardial fibrosis, particularly in the basal septum. Genetic testing reveals a heterozygous missense mutation in the *MYH7* gene.

Which of the following molecular mechanisms is most directly responsible for the pathophysiology observed in this patient?

(A) Impaired calcium reuptake by the sarcoplasmic reticulum leading to prolonged diastole.
(B) Increased contractility due to hyperphosphorylation of myosin light chains, causing excessive cross-bridge cycling.
(C) Decreased sensitivity of the cardiac troponin complex to calcium, resulting in impaired relaxation.
(D) Reduced expression of β-adrenergic receptors, leading to decreased cAMP production and reduced contractility.
(E) Increased production of reactive oxygen species (ROS) due to mitochondrial dysfunction, causing oxidative damage to cardiomyocytes.

#### Correct Answer: (B)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 45-year-old male marathon runner with sudden onset symptoms during exercise (dyspnea, chest pain, near-syncope). This suggests an acute cardiac event, potentially related to exertion.
- **Family History:** Father died suddenly at age 50 during exercise. This strongly suggests a genetic predisposition to sudden cardiac death, common in conditions like HCM or arrhythmogenic cardiomyopathy.
- **Physical Exam:** Harsh systolic murmur increasing with Valsalva (decreases preload) and decreasing with squatting (increases preload and afterload). This dynamic murmur is characteristic of LVOT obstruction, often seen in HCM.
- **ECG:** LVH and T-wave inversions are typical findings in HCM.
- **Echocardiogram:** Asymmetric septal hypertrophy (ASH), SAM of the mitral valve, and LVOT obstruction are hallmark features of obstructive HCM.
- **Cardiac MRI:** Confirms ASH and shows myocardial fibrosis, a common finding in HCM associated with increased risk of arrhythmias and sudden death.
- **Genetic Testing:** Heterozygous missense mutation in *MYH7* gene. *MYH7* encodes β-myosin heavy chain, a key component of the sarcomere. Mutations in sarcomeric proteins are the most common cause of HCM.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the *molecular mechanism* responsible for the pathophysiology.

The combination of family history, clinical presentation (exertional symptoms, dynamic murmur), ECG findings (LVH), echocardiogram findings (ASH, SAM, LVOT obstruction), and genetic testing (*MYH7* mutation) points strongly to hypertrophic cardiomyopathy (HCM). The question asks for the underlying molecular mechanism. HCM is caused by mutations in sarcomeric proteins (like β-myosin heavy chain encoded by *MYH7*, myosin-binding protein C, troponin T, etc.). These mutations lead to abnormal sarcomere function, characterized by increased cross-bridge cycling and contractility, even in the absence of adrenergic stimulation. This hyperdynamic state contributes to the hypertrophy, diastolic dysfunction, and LVOT obstruction (due to SAM).

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
- **HCM Pathophysiology:** Mutations in sarcomeric proteins (e.g., *MYH7*, *MYBPC3*, *TNNT2*, *TNNI3*, *TPM1*) disrupt the normal function of the sarcomere.
- **Molecular Mechanism:** The most common consequence of these mutations is increased interaction between actin and myosin, leading to enhanced cross-bridge cycling and increased contractility (hypercontractility). This hypercontractility is not solely dependent on adrenergic stimulation.
- **Hyperphosphorylation:** While adrenergic stimulation normally increases contractility via phosphorylation of myosin light chains (MLCs), the sarcomeric mutations in HCM often lead to a state of *constitutive* hypercontractility, meaning the sarcomere is already in a partially activated state. However, further increases in intracellular calcium or phosphorylation of MLCs (e.g., during exercise or adrenergic stimulation) can exacerbate the hypercontractility and contribute to the pathophysiology. The hypercontractility leads to impaired relaxation (diastolic dysfunction) and contributes to the hypertrophy. The increased force generation can also cause the anterior mitral valve leaflet to be pulled towards the hypertrophied septum during systole (SAM), leading to LVOT obstruction.
- **Option (B) Explanation:** Increased contractility due to hyperphosphorylation of myosin light chains (MLCs) is a key feature. While the basal state is already hypercontractile, further phosphorylation during stress (like exercise) exacerbates the problem. This hypercontractility is directly linked to the sarcomeric mutations.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Impaired calcium reuptake by the sarcoplasmic reticulum (SR) is characteristic of catecholaminergic polymorphic ventricular tachycardia (CPVT), caused by mutations in genes like *RYR2* (ryanodine receptor) or *CASQ2* (calsequestrin). This leads to calcium overload and delayed afterdepolarizations, causing arrhythmias, not the primary mechanism of HCM hypertrophy and obstruction. While diastolic dysfunction is present in HCM, it's primarily due to impaired relaxation from hypercontractility and fibrosis, not impaired calcium reuptake.
- (C): Decreased sensitivity of the cardiac troponin complex to calcium is seen in some forms of dilated cardiomyopathy or certain channelopathies, but it is not the primary mechanism in HCM. HCM is characterized by *increased* contractility, often due to mutations that enhance actin-myosin interaction, not decreased calcium sensitivity.
- (D): Reduced expression of β-adrenergic receptors can occur in heart failure or with chronic β-blocker use. It leads to decreased cAMP production and reduced contractility, which is the opposite of the hyperdynamic state seen in HCM. While β-blockers are used to *treat* HCM by reducing contractility and heart rate, the underlying disease mechanism is not reduced receptor expression.
- (E): Increased production of ROS due to mitochondrial dysfunction is implicated in various cardiomyopathies and heart failure states, contributing to oxidative stress and cellular damage. While myocardial fibrosis in HCM might involve oxidative stress, it is not the primary *molecular mechanism* driving the sarcomeric dysfunction, hypertrophy, and obstruction characteristic of HCM. The primary defect lies within the sarcomere itself.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):
| Feature             | Hypertrophic Cardiomyopathy (HCM) | Catecholaminergic Polymorphic VT (CPVT) | Dilated Cardiomyopathy (DCM) | Heart Failure (HF) |
|----------------------|-----------------------------------|----------------------------------------|--------------------------------|--------------------|
| **Primary Defect**   | Sarcomeric protein mutation       | RYR2/CASQ2 mutation (Ca2+ handling)     | Various (genetic, ischemic, etc.) | Complex, multifactorial |
| **Contractility**    | Increased (Hyperdynamic)          | Normal/Increased (arrhythmia trigger)  | Decreased                      | Decreased          |
| **Diastolic Function**| Impaired (due to stiffness/hypertrophy)| Normal                                 | Impaired                       | Impaired           |
| **Hypertrophy**      | Yes (Asymmetric Septal)           | No                                     | No (Dilated)                   | No (Dilated)       |
| **LVOT Obstruction** | Common                             | No                                     | No                             | No                 |
| **Key Mechanism**    | Increased cross-bridge cycling    | Impaired Ca2+ reuptake/release         | Myocyte loss/dysfunction       | Compensatory/decompensatory |
| **Relevant Gene**    | *MYH7*, *MYBPC3*, etc.             | *RYR2*, *CASQ2*                        | Various                        | N/A                |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
HCM is primarily caused by mutations in sarcomeric proteins, leading to increased contractility (hypercontractility) and impaired relaxation, resulting in diastolic dysfunction, hypertrophy, and potential LVOT obstruction. Differentiate HCM from CPVT (impaired Ca2+ handling, arrhythmias), DCM (dilation, decreased contractility), and HF (complex pathophysiology, often decreased contractility). Remember the dynamic murmur characteristics (increases with Valsalva, decreases with squatting) and the genetic basis involving sarcomeric proteins like β-myosin heavy chain (*MYH7*).

<a id='question-099'></a>

### Question 099: Cardiac Pathology: Focus 02-49 (Medical Genetics)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the molecular basis of hypertrophic cardiomyopathy (HCM), specifically the role of mutations in sarcomeric proteins leading to myofibril disarray and diastolic dysfunction. (理解肥厚型心肌病的分子基础，特别是肌节蛋白突变导致肌纤维紊乱和舒张功能障碍的作用。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old male competitive swimmer presents to the emergency department complaining of exertional dyspnea, palpitations, and near-syncope during a recent swim meet. He reports a family history of sudden cardiac death in his uncle at age 45 during a marathon. His past medical history is unremarkable, and he denies smoking, alcohol, or illicit drug use. Physical examination reveals a harsh systolic ejection murmur heard best at the left lower sternal border, which increases with Valsalva maneuver. His blood pressure is 120/80 mmHg, heart rate is 80 bpm, and respiratory rate is 18 breaths/min. An electrocardiogram (ECG) shows left ventricular hypertrophy (LVH) and T-wave inversions in the lateral leads. An echocardiogram reveals asymmetric septal hypertrophy (maximum wall thickness of 2.2 cm), systolic anterior motion (SAM) of the mitral valve, and left ventricular outflow tract (LVOT) obstruction with a peak gradient of 50 mmHg at rest. Cardiac MRI confirms the echocardiogram findings and shows diffuse myocardial fibrosis. Genetic testing reveals a heterozygous missense mutation in the *MYH7* gene.

Which of the following molecular mechanisms is most directly responsible for the pathological changes observed in this patient?

(A) Impaired calcium handling leading to decreased contractility and diastolic dysfunction.
(B) Disruption of actin-myosin cross-bridge formation leading to decreased force generation.
(C) Altered sarcomere structure causing myofibril disarray and impaired relaxation.
(D) Defective mitochondrial function resulting in decreased ATP production and energy failure.
(E) Increased production of reactive oxygen species (ROS) leading to oxidative stress and cardiomyocyte apoptosis.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** Young male athlete with exertional symptoms (dyspnea, palpitations, near-syncope) and family history of sudden cardiac death. This suggests a potential inherited cardiac condition.
- **Physical Exam:** Systolic ejection murmur increasing with Valsalva maneuver is characteristic of hypertrophic obstructive cardiomyopathy (HOCM), where decreased preload (Valsalva) worsens LVOT obstruction.
- **ECG:** LVH and T-wave inversions are common findings in HCM.
- **Echocardiogram:** Asymmetric septal hypertrophy, SAM of the mitral valve, and LVOT obstruction are hallmark features of HOCM.
- **Cardiac MRI:** Confirms hypertrophy and shows myocardial fibrosis, a common complication.
- **Genetic Testing:** Heterozygous missense mutation in the *MYH7* gene. *MYH7* encodes β-myosin heavy chain, a key component of the sarcomere.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the molecular mechanism responsible for the pathology.
- **Connecting Clues:** The patient has classic HOCM, confirmed by imaging and genetics (*MYH7* mutation). *MYH7* encodes a sarcomeric protein. Mutations in sarcomeric proteins are the most common cause of HCM. These mutations disrupt the normal structure and function of the sarcomere.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
- **HCM Pathophysiology:** HCM is primarily caused by mutations in genes encoding sarcomeric proteins (e.g., *MYH7*, *MYBPC3*, *TNNT2*, *TNNI3*, *TPM1*). These mutations lead to abnormal protein structure or function.
- **Molecular Mechanism:** The mutated sarcomeric proteins (like β-myosin heavy chain encoded by *MYH7*) disrupt the normal organization and function of the sarcomere. This leads to:
    - **Myofibril Disarray:** The sarcomeres are not arranged in the normal parallel fashion, causing a disorganized structure within the myofibrils.
    - **Impaired Relaxation (Diastolic Dysfunction):** The abnormal sarcomeres are stiffer and less compliant, leading to impaired ventricular relaxation and filling during diastole. This is the primary cause of symptoms in HCM.
    - **Hypertrophy:** The disorganized sarcomeres and altered signaling pathways contribute to cardiomyocyte hypertrophy, particularly asymmetric septal hypertrophy.
    - **LVOT Obstruction:** In many cases, the hypertrophy and SAM of the mitral valve cause obstruction of the left ventricular outflow tract during systole.
- **Link to *MYH7*:** The *MYH7* gene encodes the β-myosin heavy chain, which forms the thick filament of the sarcomere. Mutations here directly affect the structure and function of the contractile apparatus.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Impaired calcium handling (e.g., mutations in *RYR2*, *CACNA2dn*) can cause arrhythmias and diastolic dysfunction (catecholaminergic polymorphic VT, arrhythmogenic cardiomyopathy), but is not the primary mechanism in *MYH7*-related HCM. While diastolic dysfunction is present in HCM, the *primary* molecular defect is sarcomeric disruption, not calcium handling itself.
- (B): Disruption of actin-myosin cross-bridge formation would lead to decreased force generation and potentially dilated cardiomyopathy, not the hypertrophy and diastolic dysfunction seen in HCM. Sarcomeric mutations in HCM typically cause *increased* contractility (supercontraction) in isolated cardiomyocytes, although this is often masked by the diastolic dysfunction and obstruction in vivo.
- (D): Defective mitochondrial function (e.g., MELAS, Kearns-Sayre syndrome) leads to decreased ATP production, causing myopathies often associated with muscle weakness, exercise intolerance, and sometimes cardiomyopathy (often dilated). This is not the primary mechanism in typical HCM caused by sarcomeric mutations.
- (E): Increased ROS production and oxidative stress can contribute to cardiomyocyte damage and dysfunction in various heart diseases (e.g., ischemia, heart failure), but it is not the primary initiating molecular event in HCM caused by sarcomeric protein mutations. While oxidative stress might be a secondary consequence, the root cause is the sarcomeric defect.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):
| Feature             | Hypertrophic Cardiomyopathy (HCM) | Dilated Cardiomyopathy (DCM) | Arrhythmogenic Cardiomyopathy (ACM) | Mitochondrial Myopathy |
|----------------------|-----------------------------------|--------------------------------|--------------------------------------|------------------------|
| **Primary Defect**   | Sarcomeric protein mutation       | Various (genetic, viral, etc.) | Desmosomal protein mutations         | Mitochondrial DNA/protein |
| **Wall Thickness**   | Increased (Hypertrophy)           | Normal or Increased (Eccentric) | Normal or Thinning                  | Variable               |
| **Chamber Size**     | Normal or Small                   | Dilated                        | Normal or Dilated                    | Variable               |
| **Systolic Function**| Often Hyperdynamic (initially)    | Decreased                      | Often Normal (initially)             | Variable               |
| **Diastolic Function**| Impaired                          | Impaired                       | Impaired                             | Impaired               |
| **Key Genes**        | *MYH7*, *MYBPC3*, *TNNT2*, etc.   | *TTN*, *LMNA*, *MYH7*, etc.    | *PKP2*, *DSP*, *DSC2*, etc.          | mtDNA genes, nuclear genes |
| **Histology**        | Myofibril disarray, hypertrophy   | Myocyte hypertrophy, fibrosis | Fibrofatty replacement of myocytes | Ragged red fibers      |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
HCM is most commonly caused by mutations in genes encoding sarcomeric proteins, leading to myofibril disarray, impaired diastolic relaxation, and hypertrophy. Differentiating HCM from other cardiomyopathies relies on understanding the underlying molecular defects and resulting structural/functional changes. Key differentials include dilated cardiomyopathy (systolic dysfunction, chamber dilation) and arrhythmogenic cardiomyopathy (fibrofatty replacement, ventricular arrhythmias).

<a id='question-100'></a>

### Question 100: Cardiac Pathology: Focus 02-50 (PATHOLOGY)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the molecular mechanism of hypertrophic cardiomyopathy (HCM), specifically the role of mutations in sarcomeric proteins leading to myofibrillar disarray and diastolic dysfunction. (理解肥厚型心肌病的分子机制，特别是肌节蛋白突变导致肌纤维紊乱和舒张功能障碍。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 45-year-old male presents to the emergency department complaining of worsening shortness of breath, chest pain, and palpitations over the past week. He has a known history of hypertension, managed with lisinopril, and hyperlipidemia, treated with atorvastatin. His father died suddenly at age 50 from an apparent heart attack, although autopsy was not performed. On examination, his blood pressure is 140/90 mmHg, heart rate is 110 bpm (irregularly irregular), and respiratory rate is 24 breaths/min. Auscultation reveals a harsh systolic murmur loudest at the left lower sternal border that increases with Valsalva maneuver. An ECG shows left ventricular hypertrophy (LVH) and T-wave inversions. An echocardiogram reveals asymmetric septal hypertrophy (septal thickness 2.2 cm, free wall thickness 1.1 cm), systolic anterior motion (SAM) of the mitral valve, and left ventricular outflow tract (LVOT) obstruction with a peak gradient of 60 mmHg at rest, increasing to 90 mmHg with Valsalva. Cardiac MRI confirms the echocardiogram findings and shows extensive myocardial fibrosis, particularly in the basal septum. Genetic testing reveals a heterozygous missense mutation in the *MYH7* gene.

Which of the following molecular mechanisms is most directly responsible for the pathological changes observed in this patient?

(A) Impaired calcium handling leading to decreased contractility and diastolic dysfunction.
(B) Defective mitochondrial oxidative phosphorylation resulting in energy deficiency and myocyte hypertrophy.
(C) Disruption of sarcomere structure and function leading to myofibrillar disarray, diastolic dysfunction, and outflow tract obstruction.
(D) Accumulation of misfolded proteins causing endoplasmic reticulum stress and apoptosis of cardiomyocytes.
(E) Defective fatty acid oxidation leading to lipotoxicity and impaired energy production during stress.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Profile:** 45-year-old male with symptoms suggestive of heart failure (dyspnea, chest pain, palpitations).
*   **Family History:** Father's sudden death at a young age raises suspicion for inherited cardiac conditions.
*   **Physical Exam:** Harsh systolic murmur increasing with Valsalva is characteristic of hypertrophic obstructive cardiomyopathy (HOCM). Irregularly irregular rhythm suggests atrial fibrillation, a common complication.
*   **ECG:** LVH and T-wave inversions are consistent with HCM.
*   **Echocardiogram:** Asymmetric septal hypertrophy, SAM of the mitral valve, and LVOT obstruction are hallmark features of HOCM.
*   **Cardiac MRI:** Confirms hypertrophy and shows myocardial fibrosis, common in HCM.
*   **Genetic Testing:** Heterozygous *MYH7* gene mutation is a common cause of familial HCM. *MYH7* encodes beta-myosin heavy chain, a key component of the sarcomere.
*   **Question Stem:** Asks for the molecular mechanism responsible for the pathology.

The combination of clinical presentation (HOCM symptoms, murmur), imaging findings (asymmetric septal hypertrophy, SAM, LVOT obstruction, fibrosis), family history, and the specific *MYH7* gene mutation strongly points to HCM. The question asks for the *molecular mechanism* underlying this condition. HCM is primarily caused by mutations in genes encoding sarcomeric proteins (like *MYH7*, *MYBPC3*). These mutations disrupt the normal structure and function of the sarcomere, leading to myofibrillar disarray, impaired relaxation (diastolic dysfunction), and often hypertrophy. The disarray can also contribute to LVOT obstruction.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
*   **HCM Pathogenesis:** HCM is characterized by unexplained left ventricular hypertrophy, often asymmetric, predominantly affecting the septum. The underlying cause is frequently genetic, with mutations in genes encoding sarcomeric proteins (beta-myosin heavy chain - *MYH7*, myosin-binding protein C - *MYBPC3*, troponin T - *TNNT2*, troponin I - *TNNI3*, alpha-tropomyosin - *TPM1*, etc.).
*   **Molecular Mechanism:** These mutations alter the structure or function of sarcomeric proteins. For example, a *MYH7* mutation might affect the interaction between myosin and actin, or alter the stiffness of the myosin molecule. This disruption leads to:
    *   **Myofibrillar Disarray:** The sarcomeres are not organized properly, leading to chaotic arrangement of myofibrils within the cardiomyocytes.
    *   **Impaired Relaxation (Diastolic Dysfunction):** The abnormal sarcomeres and increased stiffness impair the heart's ability to relax and fill properly during diastole. This is often the primary functional abnormality.
    *   **Hypertrophy:** The cellular response to the sarcomeric dysfunction and increased wall stress often involves cardiomyocyte hypertrophy.
    *   **Outflow Tract Obstruction:** In some cases (HOCM), the hypertrophy, particularly of the septum, combined with SAM of the mitral valve, obstructs blood flow from the left ventricle into the aorta, especially during systole or maneuvers that decrease preload (like Valsalva).
*   **Fibrosis:** Myocardial fibrosis is also common in HCM and contributes to stiffness and arrhythmias.
*   **The *MYH7* gene:** Encodes the beta-myosin heavy chain, a critical component of the thick filament in the sarcomere. Mutations here directly affect the contractile apparatus.

Therefore, the most direct molecular mechanism responsible for the pathological changes in HCM, especially in the context of a *MYH7* mutation, is the disruption of sarcomere structure and function.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Impaired calcium handling (e.g., mutations in *RYR2*, *CACNA2dn*) can cause arrhythmias (catecholaminergic polymorphic VT) or dilated cardiomyopathy, but is not the primary mechanism in most cases of HCM, especially those linked to *MYH7*. While diastolic dysfunction is present in HCM, it's secondary to the sarcomeric defect, not primarily due to impaired calcium handling leading to decreased contractility (contractility is often normal or increased in HCM).
- (B): Defective mitochondrial oxidative phosphorylation (e.g., Kearns-Sayre syndrome, MELAS) leads to energy deficiency, which can cause various cardiomyopathies, often dilated or restrictive, but is not the characteristic mechanism of HCM linked to sarcomeric mutations. While mitochondrial dysfunction can occur secondarily in HCM, it's not the primary driver.
- (D): Accumulation of misfolded proteins causing ER stress and apoptosis is characteristic of certain proteinopathies like transthyretin amyloidosis or some forms of dilated cardiomyopathy, but not the primary mechanism of HCM caused by sarcomeric mutations. While protein misfolding can occur, the primary defect is in the sarcomeric protein itself.
- (E): Defective fatty acid oxidation (e.g., LCAD deficiency) leads to energy deficiency, particularly during fasting or stress, and can cause dilated cardiomyopathy or sudden death, but is not the mechanism underlying HCM caused by sarcomeric mutations.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):
| Feature                     | Hypertrophic Cardiomyopathy (HCM) | Dilated Cardiomyopathy (DCM) | Arrhythmogenic Cardiomyopathy (ACM) | Mitochondrial Cardiomyopathy | Calcium Handling Disorders |
| :-------------------------- | :-------------------------------- | :--------------------------- | :---------------------------------- | :--------------------------- | :------------------------- |
| **Primary Pathology**       | Myocyte hypertrophy, disarray     | Ventricular dilation, thinning | Myocyte replacement by fat/fibrosis | Variable, often dilation     | Variable                   |
| **Sarcomere Involvement**   | Yes (e.g., *MYH7*, *MYBPC3*)      | Less common                  | No                                  | No                          | No                        |
| **Diastolic Dysfunction**   | Primary                           | Secondary                    | Secondary                           | Secondary                   | Secondary                  |
| **Systolic Dysfunction**    | Often preserved or hyperdynamic    | Primary                      | Variable                            | Variable                    | Variable                   |
| **Genetic Cause Examples**  | *MYH7*, *MYBPC3*                  | *TTN*, *LMNA*                 | *PKP2*, *DSP*                        | mtDNA mutations              | *RYR2*, *CACNA2dn*          |
| **Key Feature**             | Asymmetric hypertrophy, SAM, LVOTO | Dilation, systolic failure   | Regional wall motion abnormalities | Energy deficiency            | Arrhythmias                 |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Hypertrophic cardiomyopathy (HCM) is primarily caused by mutations in genes encoding sarcomeric proteins, leading to myofibrillar disarray, diastolic dysfunction, and often hypertrophy and outflow tract obstruction. The *MYH7* gene encodes beta-myosin heavy chain. Key differentials include dilated cardiomyopathy (primary systolic dysfunction, dilation), arrhythmogenic cardiomyopathy (myocyte replacement, arrhythmias), and mitochondrial cardiomyopathies (energy deficiency). Understanding the specific genetic defect and its impact on the sarcomere is crucial for diagnosing HCM.
